@@102234274 <p> When we think of spring , we might imagine rebirth and renewal that comes with the warmer weather and longer days . It 's usually a time to celebrate , flock to spring flower festivals and spend more time in nature . <p> Spending time in nature or doing things outside , such as exercising or gardening , lifts our mood . <p> But this year , with an early start to the bushfire season , and the promise of long , hot months ahead , we may see our views about the warmer months start to shift . <p> Loading Twitter content <p> For some people , the coming months are not a celebration . They are something to fear , or feel sad about . <p> In particular , communities and emergency responders who have experienced bushfires or drought in the past may see rising levels of stress and anxiety as they face the months ahead . <h> How 's this spring different ? <p> In recent weeks , the Australian Bureau of Meteorology has declared two climate events are now under way : El Ni ? o and a positive Indian Ocean Dipole . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , as well as more intense heatwaves , bushfires and droughts . <p> Loading Twitter content <p> In temperate and subtropical regions , our summers are on average becoming hotter and longer , and winters are becoming warmer and shorter . Climate change is the primary driver of these shifts . <h> What happens to our mood as the temperature rises ? <p> Hotter temperatures and prolonged heat is linked to aggression and higher rates of emergency hospital admissions due to health conditions , heat-related injuries , and mental health concerns . <p> After an extreme weather event or disaster , rates of anxiety , depression and post-traumatic stress rise . <p> Many Australians have already experienced the psychological and physical impacts of bushfires , droughts , floods and heatwaves . <p> For some communities and individuals , experiencing these types of events may mean they are more resilient or prepared for the future . For others , the anticipation of rising heat or other climatic threats may cause concern . They may also prompt pre-traumatic stress -- the stress that comes ahead of expected loss or trauma . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prompt pre-traumatic stress . ( Reuters : David Gray ) <h> Anxiety , anger and sadness <p> As climate-related events become more widespread , people may also become increasingly affected by feelings such as anxiety , anger and sadness . <p> Climate anxiety refers to the fear , dread and worry about climate change . Anxiety can be a helpful response as it allows us to prepare and respond to future threats . For instance , climate anxiety may help prompt pro-environmental behaviour and climate action , such as attending a protest . But this type of anxiety can also become overwhelming . <p> The loss of wildlife and nature due to bushfires can leave people feeling grief over what 's lost , and anger about the lack of action to prevent these losses . <p> Losses could also be more personal , including damage to health , livelihoods , homes , or even the ability to do enjoyable outdoor activities , such as playing sports or exercising outside . <p> Another experience , solastalgia , is the " homesickness you have when you are still at home " . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ perceives negative changes and gradual deterioration to their own home environment . These feelings could arise when we notice seasonal and environmental changes to the places we love and call home . <h> But there are things you can do <p> Heading into the hotter months , strong community support , cohesion and preparedness may be especially important . There are also things you can do to maintain and manage your mental health and wellbeing . Though more research is needed to understand which strategies work best , health professionals suggest : <p> connecting with others , especially people you trust and who support your wellbeing <p> finding ways to connect with your community either in person ( for example , through community gardening ) or online ( for example , via discussion groups ) <p> being mindful of your physical and psychological safety ( for instance , especially during climate-related events ) and , if you need it , seeking professional support <p> taking a break from distressing media content when needed . <p> Understandably , people may continue to be anxious about the seasons to come with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ overwhelmed , you can also respond to and channel your distressing feelings . You can take part in community-led climate action projects , and spend time outdoors and in nature ( even for short bursts of time ) . <p> These actions might help uphold the positive links between wellbeing and nature , no matter the season . <p> If this article has raised issues for you , or if you 're concerned about someone you know , call Lifeline on 13 11 14 . <p> Tara Crandon is a psychologist and PhD candidate at the QIMR Berghofer Medical Research Institute . This piece first appeared on The Conversation . 
@@102234279 <p> Earlier this year it also made headlines for helping deliver a rare dry-season downpour across outback Australia . <p> The jet streams have been described by some scientists , including Oxford University professor of climate science Tim Woollings , as " arguably the greatest weather systems on Earth " . <p> So what exactly are they and how do they affect the weather ? <h> The barrier between cold and warm air <p> The jet stream forms where the cold air of the poles meets the relatively warm air of the equator . <p> The stronger the temperature difference , the stronger the winds , which reach up to 400 kilometres per hour during the winter months . <p> Loading YouTube content <p> There are two main jet streams that circle the globe in each hemisphere -- the subtropical jet , which in the Southern Hemisphere sits at about the same latitude as Perth in WA to Bourke in New South Wales , and the polar jet , which is over the Southern Ocean . <p> These winds tend to meander and snake their way around the globe in a waving pattern . <p> You 've likely come @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Flight times from west to east can be cut by up to a third if pilots get into the jet stream . ( Unsplash : Rae Galatas / licence ) <p> When travelling from west to east , pilots try and get into the jet stream for an extra push , like paddling downstream on a river . This is why it 's often quicker flying from west to east than the other way around . <p> Australia is influenced by both jet streams , but the subtropical jet is only present over Australia during the cooler months of the year , dissipating during summer . <h> Links to extreme weather <p> Despite being located well above the surface , the jet stream directly impacts the weather on the ground in a number of ways , particularly in southern Australia , according to Monash University and ARC Centre of Excellence for Climate Extremes meteorologist Michael Barnes . <p> " Anything from a cold front that brings us rain to a heatwave in south-eastern Australia can be associated with the jet stream , its position and the waves moving along @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ does so is by helping steer mid-latitude weather systems , like lows and fronts , across the globe from west to east like a conveyor belt . <p> It can enhance the strength of weather systems at the surface , such as low-pressure systems . <p> An omega block set up September 's floods in Greece and Spain . ( AP : George Kidonas/InTime News ) <p> At times -- when it breaks -- it can also lead to weather systems being stuck over one location for days on end , leading to extreme heat or rain . <p> However Dr Barnes said the waves propagating along the jet occasionally became amplified to the point when they " break " like waves in the ocean . <p> With that conveyor belt broken , he said this could mean a high-pressure system dominates , stalled in one spot with nothing to help move it along , ultimately causing a traffic jam in weather systems behind it . <p> The Northern Hemisphere is more prone to blocking systems , as its jet streams are wavier due to there being more land mass @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> While their impact on the weather is significant , the history of how the jet stream was discovered has all the makings of a Hollywood movie -- featuring the aeronauts and attempts to use it as a weapon of war . <p> The age of the aeronauts saw daring scientists take to new heights . ( Supplied : Wikimedia Commons ) <p> The jet was first observed in the late 1800s when weather forecasting was in its infancy and celebrity scientists would take on dangerous balloon ascents of ever-increasing heights in a bid to better understand the atmosphere . <p> On a voyage in 1864 , James Glaisher made note of a warm , south-westerly wind blowing high up in the atmosphere . <p> He was right . But it was n't until the 1940s , in the midst of World War II , that the knowledge of the jet stream really took hold . <p> Flying their high-altitude bombers , American forces were caught off guard as they struggled to line up targets in the unbelievably strong winds and chewed through fuel as they flew into the headwind @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at seven miles ' while colleague Henry Coxwell untangles a trapped rope with his teeth . ( Supplied : Wikimedia Commons ) <p> Meanwhile the Japanese , who had learnt of the winds decades earlier , were attempting to use the jet stream as a weapon , albeit with not much success . <p> They launched more than 9,000 hydrogen-filled balloon bombs with the idea they would catch a lift across the globe on the jet stream and drop on North America . <p> Due to errors in the calculations , however , only 300 of these bombs made the distance -- one killed a pregnant woman and five children in Oregon . <p> Japanese meteorologist Wasaburo Oishi is largely credited with discovering the jet stream , however his research failed to spread very far at the time . <p> This was partly due to it being written in the experimental " international language " Esperanto that , somewhat ironically , had n't really caught on . <h> Changes in the jet stream <p> In Australia , the further south the jet stream is , the further away the rain-baring @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ parts of the country get . <p> Meteorologist Milton Speer , visiting fellow from the University of Technology Sydney 's School of Mathematical and Physical Sciences , said this was what had been happening in recent decades due to global warming . <p> " The jet streams have in fact shifted about five degrees south , " he said . <p> " That means there 's been less of those low-pressure systems that give rain to inland south-east Australia , like Victoria , and in southern New South Wales there has been less rain in the cool season months there . " <p> Dr Speer said there were also theories that the reduced contrast in temperature between the Arctic and the equator was making the Northern Hemisphere jet streams weaker and " wavier " , resulting in " long-lived extremes of temperature and rainfall " . <p> This is still at the frontier of climate science , however , so there remains a lot of uncertainty around the exact influence of climate change on the jet stream . <p> Some recent research also found that flight turbulence had increased on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as climate change has warmed the planet .

@@102234408 <h> Save articles for later <p> I 'm in Yokohama , Japan , slightly dazed . I 've just spent 55 seconds strapped into a chair that moved with the ferocity of a magnitude-8 earthquake . <p> " It was very fun and enjoyable , " said eight-year-old Akari , who had given the chair a whirl before I did . " But if it happened in real life , it would be really bad . " Wise words . <p> Shaken , and stirred . <p> Those few seconds made me understand how powerless a human is when a violent earthquake strikes ; how your prospects for survival depend on the preparations you 've made for such a moment . <p> The simulation was set up by one of the agriculture-focused JA Group 's cooperatives at the National Disaster Resilience Congress in Yokohama . It aims to educate the public about what to do in the increasingly likely event of a natural disaster , including typhoons , tsunamis , earthquakes and landslides . <p> Their experience underlined everything I 've learnt during my week touring the country as one of 15 fellows selected by the Japan Foundation and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ across the Indo-Pacific . <p> Leading by example ... Japanese Prime Minister Fumio Kishida took part in a joint disaster drill in Kanagawa prefecture on September 1 . Credit : AP <p> Japan sits on four continental plates , is home to 111 active volcanoes and experiences about 10 per cent of the world 's earthquakes . But boy does it prepare . Australia -- already experiencing the more intense and frequent fires and floods brought by climate change -- can and should learn much from Japan , urgently . A starting point for both government and media should be a national conversation to embed disaster preparedness into our national psyche . <p> In Japan , the official messaging runs in this order : " self help " , " mutual help " then " public help . " <p> Advertisement <p> Perhaps it 's okay to be " a bit of a busybody " sometimes , quipped Yuji Kuroiwa , Governor of Kanagawa , as he outlined how the strength of a response to a disaster lies in the community and encouraged neighbours to get to know one another @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this through tragic and bitter experiences . For example , Minamisanriku Town , ground zero of the 2011 tsunami , no longer exists . Those who survived moved inland . It is hard , visiting the area today , to imagine how people ever felt safe living on the edge of the Pacific in such an earthquake-prone country . <p> The mangled remains of the former Disaster Measures government building at the Minamisanriku Town Earthquake Recovery Memorial Park , Japan.Credit : Latika Bourke <p> Two buildings speak to the town 's destruction . The first is the mangled metal remains of the one that served as the disaster warning centre . The second is the Minamisanriku 311 Memorial , which honours those who lost their lives and serves as an educational resource , inviting visitors to consider their own suite of survival skills and evacuation plans . <p> I was struck by the sight of Australian flags , prominently displayed at its entrance and front desk . Australia was the first foreign country to send rescuers and aid after the tsunami and then prime minister Julia Gillard was the first @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of the Australian rescuers sits in a glass cabinet . <p> Japan built not just memorials but also museums to mark their most catastrophic earthquakes . Although controversial , they are thought to provide a positive and instructional way to channel the collective PTSD . <p> Nowhere was this better demonstrated than at the foot of the cement ruins of Okawa Elementary School . The school sits just a few hundred metres from the base of a hill easy enough for any child to climb . <p> The remains of the Okawa Elementary School that was hit by the 3/11 tsunami.Credit : Latika Bourke <p> But despite having plenty of advance warning that the tsunami was coming , the children were held on lower ground in the schoolyard and those who attempted to escape were chastised . Above all , they are taught to save their own lives first . Akari told me how she regularly practises earthquake drills at her school and I had no doubt she knew exactly what to do should something happen . <p> Loading <p> If Australia wants to get serious about how it deals with disaster , it 's going to have to accept the costs of this level of permanent readiness . In Japan , the sums have saved lives . <p> The country 's Self Defence Forces have 3900 people , 1100 vehicles and 40 aircraft on standby , deployable within an hour . Similarly , a cabinet minister is always available to attend , within 30 minutes , the emergency meeting that will be activated when disaster strikes . <p> Representatives from private sector companies ( think infrastructure , communications , utility companies ) are seconded to the cabinet office to join the officials who work on the response plans . <p> Relevant legislation is constantly updated and laws have even been passed to allow @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Disaster Reduction Institute we learnt that drivers are told to leave their keys in the ignition if they have to abandon their vehicle , so emergency workers can move it . <p> It all felt a far cry from Australia 's approach which , having established that Defence ca n't be first port of call to respond to our floods and fires , has only this week held its first National Disaster Preparedness Summit and is still to work out just who will step in if the military ca n't . <p> To understand Japan is to appreciate that the backdrop to daily life is its constant state of readiness . As one Australian who works in disaster response summed it up , disaster preparedness is embedded in its psyche . <p> Indeed , as I sat down to listen to the US ambassador to Japan deliver his speech , the host read out the evacuation procedures and with a smile , added " just in case " . This could easily be Japan 's national motto . Australia would do well to think similarly , stepping away from the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ blights its ability to think long-term . <p> Latika Bourke is a journalist for The Sydney Morning Herald and The Age , based in London . @@102263425 <h> The road is long and time is short , but Australia 's pace towards net zero is quickening <p> The marks of industry have forever changed the Hunter Valley in New South Wales , edged by the Blue Mountains to the south and ancient rainforests to the north . <p> Coal has been mined here for more than 200 years , providing generations of people with good livelihoods and lives . But the end of coal in the Hunter does not spell the end of communities . Quite the opposite . <p> The Hunter is developing a clean manufacturing precinct , and state and federal governments are investing heavily in the effort . Projects to create hydrogen and renewable energy to replace coal and gas are underway . <p> At Kooragang Island , just north of Newcastle , Orica and the Australian government are working together to change what the exhaust stacks at three nitric acid manufacturing plants put into the air . Nitrous oxide , a greenhouse gas now released directly into the atmosphere , is being converted into nitrogen and oxygen , and emissions at these plants are set to halve . <p> In the Hunter-Central @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Waratah Super Battery , likely to be the Southern Hemisphere 's largest standby network battery , is among the renewable energy projects that will replace the Eraring coal-fired power station when it closes in 2025 . Even in the Hunter , with its long fossil fuel history , change is in the air . <h> Positive action is happening <p> Pessimism abounds about the world 's capacity to solve climate change , but as chief executive of Climateworks Centre , which works with governments , companies , regulators and investors to create pathways to net zero emissions , I see evidence every day of people doing hard practical work to bring their organisations into line with our national and global climate goals . <p> The road is long and time is short , but our pace is quickening . The price of renewables is falling , coal-fired power plants are sunsetting sooner , hydrogen technology is progressing . And as industries and economies change , so too will minds . <p> The ambition of climate action must continue to aim at a global temperature increase of no more than 1.5 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exponentially worse outcomes . Last week , the US Treasury advised that financial institutions ' net zero plans should be in line with a 1.5 ? pathway . <p> In 2020 when my organisation 's climate modelling with CSIRO showed the first 1.5 ? -aligned path to net zero for Australia , the Paris Agreement , which committed the world to strive for such a pathway , was five years old . <p> The progress in technology in that time demonstrated potential to bring emissions to net zero over a decade faster than previously shown . <p> Image : AAP/James Ross . A third of Australia 's emissions are from agriculture and transport . <h> Australia 's next steps are obvious <p> Australia 's emissions are roughly a third from electricity , a third from mining , manufacturing , construction and buildings , and a third from transport and agriculture . So the path is clear . <p> Switch anything that runs on fossil fuels to electricity or , in a few cases , to a zero-emissions substitute such as green hydrogen or biofuel . Generate all electricity from renewable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In short , it 's a technology upgrade across the economy plus investment in nature-based solutions that protect and restore ecosystems and sustainably manage agricultural land and forests . <p> At the same time , improve energy efficiency , a stealthy superpower at our disposal . Already , digitalisation is making everything from home thermostats to long-haul trucking more efficient . More stars are cropping up on home appliance energy rating labels . <p> Building standards are improving home energy performance . Victoria has said all new homes will be fully electric from next year . With a clear view of what is needed to bring homes to net zero , other states and the federal government can follow its lead . <p> As for electrification , even some of the trickiest switches from fossil fuels to renewable energy are underway . The Australian Industrial Energy Transitions Initiative , a group of heavy industry and business leaders co-convened by Climateworks Centre , shows iron and steel , aluminium and chemical supply chains , home to many of Australia 's " hard-to-abate " emissions , can cut between 96 per @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while increasing production to meet the needs of a net-zero global economy . <p> Until recently , electric and renewable processes simply could not generate as much heat as fossil fuels to power these industries . Take aluminium , which begins as bauxite , a reddish-brown rock very common in Australia . <p> To be transformed into aluminium , bauxite must undergo a process called digestion at around 200 ? , followed by calcination at around 1,000 ? , before it 's smelted into its final silvery state . <p> For a long time , no renewable process could get anywhere near 1,000 ? . But near Gladstone earlier this year the world 's first hydrogen calcination demonstration pilot was announced at Rio Tinto 's Yarwun Alumina Refinery in Queensland . The pilot , funded by the Australian Renewable Energy Agency , aims to prove hydrogen calcination is viable for industrial use at scale . <p> Image : Jono Searle/AAP Image . Switching to electrified transport will be necessary as part of the energy transition . <p> Success would be no small thing ; this one industrial process alone is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ emissions . Decarbonising both calcination and digestion would cut up to three times that amount . <p> Yet we are out of time to work on all this incrementally . <p> A few big changes are needed now . One is to deploy technologies across entire supply chains and across new , net zero precincts , with companies and sectors pulling together to achieve more than they can on their own . <h> Layers of connectivity are required for the next big push <p> Over the next year the federal government is also developing net zero plans for six sectors : electricity and energy , industry , resources , the built environment , agriculture , and land transport . Done properly , they will be a big deal in getting us to net zero . <p> A holistic approach can solve multiple goals at once : electrify , build renewable power , support communities and protect nature . This must occur in the industrial regions that power Australia 's economy . Five of them -- the Pilbara , Kwinana , Hunter , Illawarra and Gladstone -- contribute more than $160 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the equivalent of nearly half the value of Australia 's exported goods . <p> Industrial facilities in these regions can be brought together within a renewable energy industrial precinct . The idea is simple -- supercharge industrial areas with plenty of renewable energy , green hydrogen , shared infrastructure , labour and knowledge . Coordinate the public policy and investment that will attract further private sector investment , supporting the transition of the existing and creation of the new at the same time . <p> These precincts will provide a path to prosperity for workers and industries , built on demand for the renewable energy that will underpin Australia 's ambitions to be an energy superpower produces green iron , green hydrogen and other products the world urgently wants . <p> The Net Zero Authority -- created in July to help " seize the opportunities of Australia 's net zero transformation " -- is the layer of connective tissue that will help these precincts succeed . The Authority will coordinate policies to support regions and communities to attract clean energy industries , and help investors and companies to take @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Australians have a role to play , in our actions and in our minds . At Climateworks we say , " start at the end goal " . Imagine a prosperous net zero society 30 years from now . <p> Huge industrial regions feature battery blocks , not exhaust stacks . Cities hum , more quietly than today , with electric vehicles . Homes are warmed and cooled by the power of the sun , and we breathe fresh air . <p> Once we put ourselves in that future , and look around at the infrastructure and relationships needed to create it , the rest of the equation can -- and I believe will -- be solved . <p> We are still far from that ideal , but the turning point , when it comes , will be swift . Australia has an enormous opportunity in decarbonising quickly , in a world that we know wants the same . 
@@102286472 <h> Aussie uni students built these incredible solar-powered cars <p> Australian university students will compete with teams from across the globe for a first-place finish at the 2023 Bridgestone World Solar Challenge ( BWSC ) , an annual event in which participants race their own solar-powered cars across the Outback . <p> Teams of secondary and tertiary students are challenged to travel through 3000 kilometres from Darwin to Adelaide in their own self-designed , self-engineered and self-built solar-powered vehicles . <p> The challenge is centred on providing solutions for the pressing global issue of delivering sustainable transport options . <p> Since its inception in 1987 , the biennial event has become a testing ground for significant solar technology developments powered by the unforgiving outback sun . <p> In 2019 , the BWSC had its record year , with 53 entries from 24 countries , 1500 participants and over 25 million in global audience -- the event is looking to bounce back this year after a four-year hiatus during the COVID-19 pandemic . <p> For a taste of what 's to come , we 've rounded up the entries from the Australian teams competing at this year 's challenge , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ October 29 . <h> Sunswift 7 -- University of NSW <p> The Sunswift 7 made headlines back in December 2022 when it completed 1000km on a single charge in under 12 hours . <p> " Our approach to designing Sunswift 7 was performance optimisation across all elements with a focus on efficiency , innovation and safety , " said a spokesperson for the UNSW Sydney team . <p> " We wanted Sunswift 7 to not look out of place in a new car showroom , proving that you can blend ultimate aerodynamics and style . " <p> UNSW allows undergraduate students who compete in the BWSC to gain course credit through their participation , and has enabled a greater level of collaboration with various industry technology partners -- as shown by the Sunswift 7 's ability to be driven remotely across the outback using Optus 's 5G network . <p> " We are going to be broadcasting the race live from our car in the middle of the desert all the way back to Sydney using 5G mobile , " said Sunswift Racing Team Principal , Professor Richard Hopkins . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on campus at the university checking all the telemetry and data from the car . @@102286872 <p> Embarking from Puerto Princesa , the Subterranean River National Park is more than just a UNESCO World Heritage site . Delve deep into this natural wonder where an experienced guide paddles visitors through a labyrinthine cave system , with the river stretching over eight kilometers beneath the St. Paul Mountain Range . <p> The echoing chambers , illuminated sporadically , unveil dramatic stalactite and stalagmite formations , each narrating tales of time . The subtle sounds of the water , intermingled with the distant chirps of the cave-dwelling swiftlets , creates an ambiance that 's both eerie and enchanting . Beyond the river , the park is a biodiversity hotspot , making it a haven for wildlife enthusiasts keen on spotting monitor lizards , macaques , and a myriad of bird species . <p> 2 . Island Hopping in Honda Bay <p> A stone 's throw away from Puerto Princesa , Honda Bay glistens like a sapphire under the tropical sun . This package is not just an island-hopping tour but a journey into marine wonder . <p> The day commences at Pambato Reef , where the underwater world unravels its magic . Vivid corals , clownfishes , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Starfish Island , true to its name , is a sanctuary for countless starfishes , each sprawled on the sandy bed or gracefully moving about . Luli Island , known for its vanishing sandbar , offers a delightful beachfront lunch , where the fresh catch of the day is grilled to perfection , seasoned with local spices and served with a side of panoramic ocean views . <p> 3 . Forest Canopy Zipline Adventure <p> For those whose hearts beat faster with a touch of adventure , the verdant forests surrounding Puerto Princesa offer an exhilarating zipline experience . <p> This package introduces participants to a bird's-eye view of the dense canopy , crisscrossed by streams and inhabited by diverse fauna . <p> As one glides from one platform to another , the wind rushing past and the ground a blur of green below , there 's an unmatched sense of freedom . <p> Expert guides ensure safety while sharing tidbits about the ecological significance of the region . <p> Post the zipline rush , a leisurely trek through the forest allows for a grounded exploration , with chances of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dwellers . <p> 4 . Mangrove Paddle Boat Tour in Sabang <p> A world away from the bustling tourist trails , the serene mangroves of Sabang beckon with their intricate ecosystems . <p> This package unveils the wonders of these tidal forests in a traditionally crafted bamboo paddle boat . <p> Guided by locals with generational knowledge of the area , the journey becomes a learning experience . <p> As the boat gently navigates the quiet waterways , one is surrounded by towering mangrove trees , their roots arching above the water in a fascinating display of nature 's resilience . <p> The echoing calls of mudskippers , monitor lizards basking in the sun , and the occasional sight of tree snakes add to the allure . <p> And then there 's the symphony - the rhythmic chorus of the mangrove crickets , punctuated by bird calls . This tour not only educates visitors about the crucial role of mangroves in coastal ecology but also supports community-based sustainable tourism . <p> This expedition is an ode to the region 's unmatched beauty . Beginning with a sunrise breakfast on a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , teeming with marine life and framed by ancient cliffs . <p> The journey weaves through secret caves , where stalactites glisten , leading to hidden lagoons that change hues with the sun 's movement . <p> Lunch is an elaborate affair on Entalula Beach , where chefs conjure up a feast from the day 's fresh catch , complemented by tropical fruits and local delicacies . <p> As the day winds down , participants are taken to the peak of Taraw Cliff , a vantage point from where El Nido looks like a painting come to life , with the golden glow of sunset casting ethereal reflections on the waters below . <p> 6 . Palawan Wildlife Rescue and Conservation Center Visit <p> This package offers a blend of wildlife observation and conservation awareness . <p> The center , located in Puerto Princesa , is home to the Philippine crocodile , a critically endangered species . Here , visitors can safely observe these majestic reptiles from viewing decks . <p> Beyond the crocodiles , the center also houses a mini-zoo with Palawan bearcats , porcupines , and other native @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ experts enlightens visitors about the importance of biodiversity conservation , rehabilitation of rescued wildlife , and the challenges these creatures face due to habitat destruction and climate change . <p> A walk through the nature trail , flanked by endemic plants , is both relaxing and educational , introducing guests to the region 's rich flora . <p> 7 . Culion Historical and Cultural Tour <p> While nature is the undeniable highlight of the Puerto Princesa to El Nido corridor , the islands also harbor tales of human resilience and history . <p> Culion , once the largest leper colony in the world , today stands as a symbol of triumph over adversity . <p> This package begins with a visit to the Culion Museum and Archives , where old photographs , documents , and artifacts narrate the island 's poignant past . <p> The tour then moves to scenic spots , including the Aguila viewpoint , offering panoramic views of Coron Bay . <p> The culmination is a visit to the old church , where echoes of the past intermingle with the faith and hope of the present . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a golden hue on the tranquil waters of Palawan , it 's time to reflect upon a journey that transcends typical tourist trails . <p> The pathway from Puerto Princesa to El Nido is not merely a route on a map , but a voyage through time , ecology , and human spirit . <p> Each destination we explored , every story we unraveled , reminds us of the intricate bond we share with nature and history . <p> Palawan 's allure lies not just in its picture-perfect landscapes but in its ability to make us ponder , dream , and appreciate the delicate balance of life . <p> As we leave behind the echoes of the waves , the whispering forests , and the tales of resilient communities , let 's carry with us a piece of Palawan . <p> A piece that reminds us to travel with intention , to seek understanding , and to cherish the beautiful symphony of nature and culture that our world offers .

@@102289769 <p> The great-power competition between the United States and China is a defining feature of the first part of this century , but there 's little agreement on how it should be characterised . Some call it an ' enduring rivalry ' analogous to the one between Germany and Britain prior to the last century 's two world wars . Others worry that America is like Sparta ( the dominant power ) and China is like Athens ( the rising power ) in the 5th century BC : the two are ' destined for war ' . The problem , of course , is that a belief in the inevitability of conflict can become a self-fulfilling prophecy . <p> ' Enduring rivalry ' itself is a misleading term . Just think of all the phases the Sino-American relationship has gone through since the Chinese Communist Party came to power in 1949 . In the 1950s , American and Chinese soldiers were killing each other on the Korean peninsula . In the 1970s , after US President Richard Nixon 's historic visit to China , the two countries cooperated closely to counterbalance the Soviet Union . In the 1990s , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ entry into the World Trade Organization . Not until after 2016 did we enter the current phase of great-power competition , with one US official describing China as a ' pacing threat ' -- meaning ' the only country that can pose a systemic challenge ' to America ' economically , technologically , politically and militarily . ' <p> But even if enduring rivalry does n't imply violent conflict , what about a ' cold war ' ? If that term refers to an intense prolonged competition , we are already in one . But if it 's a historical analogy , the comparison is inapt , and risks misleading us about the real challenges the US faces from China . The US and the Soviet Union had a high level of global military interdependence , but virtually no economic , social or ecological interdependence . Today 's Sino-American relationship is different in all those dimensions . <p> For starters , America ca n't decouple its trade and investment completely from China without causing enormous damage to itself and the global economy . Moreover , the US and its allies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but by a system of economic and political interdependence that both sides routinely manipulate . Partial decoupling or ' de-risking ' on security issues is necessary , but total economic decoupling would be prohibitively costly , and few US allies would follow suit . More countries count China rather than the US as their leading trade partner . <p> Then there are the ecological aspects of interdependence , which make decoupling impossible . No country can tackle climate change , the pandemic threat or other transnational problems alone . For better and worse , we are locked in a ' cooperative rivalry ' with China , in need of a strategy that can advance contradictory objectives . The situation is nothing like Cold War containment . <p> Meeting the China challenge will require an approach that leverages the alliances and rules-based system the US created . Allies like Japan , and partners like India , are assets that China lacks . Although the center of global economic gravity has shifted from Europe to Asia over the past century , India , the world 's most populous country , is one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ' global south ' or solidarity among the BRICS ( Brazil , Russia , India , China and South Africa ) are highly misleading , because they ignore internal rivalries within those categories . Moreover , the combined wealth of Western democratic allies will far exceed that of China ( plus Russia ) well into this century . <p> To succeed , America 's China strategy must set realistic goals . If the US defines strategic success as transforming China into a Western democracy , it is likely to fail . The CCP fears Western liberalisation , and China is too big to invade or fundamentally change through coercion . This reality cuts both ways : the US has domestic problems , but they certainly do n't owe anything to the attractiveness of Chinese communism . In this important respect , neither China nor the US poses an existential threat to the other -- unless they blunder into a major war . <p> The best historical analogy is not Cold War Europe after 1945 but pre-war Europe in 1914 . European leaders welcomed what they thought would be a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ four terrible years of World War I. Some foresee the US and China blundering into a similar war over Taiwan , which China regards as a renegade province . When Nixon and Mao Zedong met in 1972 , they could not agree on this issue , but they devised a rough formula for managing it that has lasted half a century : no de jure independence for Taiwan , and no use of force against the island by China . Maintaining the status quo requires deterring Beijing while avoiding the provocation of supporting de jure independence for Taiwan . War is a risk , but it is not inevitable . <p> The US should expect low-intensity economic conflicts with China , but its strategic objectives should be to avoid escalation -- what US Secretary of State Antony Blinken recently called ' peaceful coexistence ' . That means using deterrence to avoid a hot war , cooperating when possible , leveraging US hard and soft power to attract allies , and marshalling domestic assets to compete successfully . The goal should be to shape China 's external behaviour by strengthening America @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the key to advancing US interests in the South and East China Seas is Japan , a close ally that hosts US troops . But since the US also needs to bolster its own economic and technological advantages , it would be wise to adopt a more active Asian trade policy , and to offer assistance to the low- and middle-income countries being wooed by China . Global polls suggest that if the US maintains its domestic openness and democratic values , it will have much greater soft power than China . <p> Investments in America 's own military power of deterrence are welcomed by the many countries that want to maintain trade relations with China but do n't want to be dominated by it . If the US maintains its alliances and avoids demonisation and misleading historical analogies , ' cooperative rivalry ' will be a sustainable goal .

@@102317406 <p> From 2002 , when China joined the World Trade Organisation ( WTO ) , the AUD rose considerably as China built a modern economy using Aussie commodities . It did so by siphoning off developed market industrial complexes . So , they , in turn , had weak currencies versus the AUD . <p> However , since 2011 , that dynamic has reversed . Other developed markets have rebuilt industrial bases while Australia has not : <p> Developed economies like the US are , once again , becoming more diverse , self-sufficient and sophisticated . <p> This has the flow-on effect of lifting productivity and the labour share of income , which boosts growth and , ultimately , a currency . <p> Conversely , Australia has chosen to grow its population via immigration , rather than its industrial base . <p> This has the opposite effect . It weighs on wages , lifts input costs like land , and is disproductive as capital ( machines , infrastructure etc ) is shallowed across more people . <p> In effect , we get more people but less output growth per person as the economy gets less sophisticated . <p> Why @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ twenty underpaid workers to scrub your hubcaps ? <p> The end game <p> The great superpower transition is still ongoing . Its technology firms have seized leadership in artificial intelligence , guaranteeing further capital deepening in sophisticated value-added growth . <p> So , the two forces that brought the Australian dollar down from $1.11 to today 's 0.63 cents are ongoing . @@102317638 <h> Opinion <h> Save articles for later <p> Add articles to your saved list and come back to them any time . <p> Advertisement <p> For all our lives , worthies -- our politicians , business people and economists -- have assured us we need economic growth to make us better off . Almost everything I write assumes this to be true . But is it ? <p> These days , there are more doubters than there used to be . Some people do n't believe that spending your life striving to own more stuff will make you happy . ( Spoiler : they 're right . ) <p> Illustration : Simon Letch.Credit : <p> But a growing number of scientists tell us unending growth in the economy simply is n't physically possible , and the more we keep growing the more we 'll damage the natural environment , to our great cost . Climate change is just the most glaring example of the damage we 're doing . <p> Historians remind us that our obsession with The Economy is relatively recent . It did n't take hold until the middle of last century . <p> What we call @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's people getting up every morning , going out to earn a living , and then spending what they 've earned . So it 's " getting and spending " , production and consumption . This is measured by gross domestic product -- the value of all the goods and services produced during a period . <p> It was only when we started regularly measuring GDP in the mid-1950s that we began our obsession with whether it was growing and by how much . Or whether -- God forfend -- it was going backwards . <p> But these are just modern words and concepts . In truth , Australians have been preoccupied by what today we call economic growth since the day white people arrived . Their magic words were " settlement " , " progress " and " nation building " . <p> The recent arrivals saw a " new " nation where nothing had been done , but with huge potential for endless bush to be made to resemble the old country . They set about clearing the land , damming rivers , building houses , establishing farms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ To become more prosperous . They spurred themselves on with the belief they must " populate or perish " -- be taken over by invading Asians . <p> Loading <p> So how do you achieve what we call economic growth ? The easiest way is to grow the population . Have lots of kids and encourage ( in those days , white-only ) immigration . That gives you more people to work , but also more people needing to be fed , clothed , housed and entertained . <p> Bingo . A bigger economy . But while increasing the population makes the economy , GDP , bigger , it 's really only if the growth increases GDP per person that it can be claimed to make us better off , to have raised our material standard of living . And this does n't follow automatically . <p> The harder way to grow the economy is to increase the proportion of the population in paid employment , or to increase our investment in plant and equipment , and ( well-chosen ) public infrastructure . This does increase GDP per person . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increase GDP per person . It 's to take the same quantity of resources -- raw materials , labour and capital equipment -- and use them to produce more output of goods and services than you did . <p> This is what people mean when they talk about increasing our " productivity " . It 's achieved , as economists keep repeating , by " working smarter , not working harder " . <p> Climate change is just the most glaring example of the damage we 're doing.Credit : Bloomberg <p> How ? By building a better educated and more skilled workforce , and by finding ways to make the organisation of factories and offices more efficient , but mainly by advances in technology that create better machines ( and these days , computer programs ) doing better tricks . <p> This is the bit scientists do n't get . When they hear the word " growth " they think of one thing : growth in humans ' exploitation of natural resources and all the damage we do to the environment in the process . <p> But that 's not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ growth in GDP over the long term comes from increased productivity , not increased inputs of raw materials , labour and physical capital . <p> This means that , unless you believe there 's a limit to human ingenuity , it 's not true that continuing growth in GDP is impossible . <p> Loading <p> But when scientists say more clearly the kind of " growth " they 're referring to -- growth in the use of natural resources and " ecosystem services " -- it 's not possible to argue with the laws of physics . <p> While economists used to argue that the " limits to growth " were n't as close at hand as some scientists had calculated , the possibility of the developing world enjoying the same profligate use of natural resources as the rich world is not credible . We expect the bottom 80 per cent to resign themselves to lives of relative poverty , while we in the top 20 per cent continue partying as though there 's no tomorrow . <p> So I accept that we and other rich countries will have to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if the planet is to remain functional . A " circular economy " in which resources are so expensive that almost everything has to be repaired , reused and recycled ? Sure . <p> But here 's the joke . It would n't be the scientists who worked out how we could move to such an economy , it would be the economists .

@@102346791 <h> Australian battery recycling start-up given $8 million to scale tech <p> Perth-based company Renewable Metals has been given $8 million in funding to scale up its new battery recycling technology . <p> The money has been invested by the Clean Energy Finance Corporation ( CEFC ) , through companies Virescent Ventures and Investible . <p> Renewable Metals says can extract over 95% of the lithium , as well as high amounts of nickel , cobalt , copper and manganese from lithium-ion batteries without having to finely grind the batteries into a substance called ' black mass ' . <p> " Usually what companies will do is use sulfuric acid , then they need to add something like sodium hydroxide to neutralise the acid , " said Renewable Metals CEO Luan Atkinson . <p> " When they do that , they create sodium sulphate . Usually there 'll be 1.5 tonnes of sodium sulphate produced for every tonne of battery being recycled , which is really significant . " <p> " Our process does n't produce any chemically precipitated byproducts . " <p> The funds will be used to continue running a pilot plant in Perth , WA , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which can recycle 1,500 tonnes of battery waste a year . Atkinson told RenewEconomy that the team are hoping to have the demonstration plant up and running in about 12 months . <p> According to the CSIRO , Australia produced about 3,300 tonnes of lithium-ion battery waste in 2020 but this has probably already expanded . <p> Research done earlier this year by the University of Technology Sydney and commissioned by the Battery Stewardship Council found that 30,000 tonnes of used electric vehicle batteries will enter the waste stream in Australia by 2030 . <p> " Based on conversations we 've had with people in the industry , we think that there should be enough to feed the 1500 tonne per annum plant , " said Atkinson . <p> " Then scale up to between 5,000 and 15,000 towards the back end of the decade , to meet the volume that 's coming . " <p> Currently , most of Australia 's battery waste that does n't end up in landfill is turned into black mass in Australia before being shipped overseas . Refining it in Australia could double or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recycling that extracts valuable metals and materials is an important part of building Australia 's circular economy as demand for batteries grows , " said Virescent Ventures Partner , Blair Pritchard . <p> " By developing end-of-life battery systems , Australia can participate across the battery value chain , from critical minerals extraction , refining , processing operation and maintenance and the eventual repurposing and recycling of batteries and components . " <p> In the next few years the world will likely see more of a shortage of lithium and other critical minerals as more EVs and battery storage are required to transistion away from fossil fuels . <p> While recycling batteries over and over again in a circular system will be critical in the coming decades , an infinite loop of battery use and recycling is still a while away yet . <p> " Funding for Renewable Metals is the kind of thing the CEFC was made for , giving Australian companies a boost and helping finance clean energy innovation and groundbreaking technology as the world transforms to a net zero economy , " says Minister for Climate Change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is good for jobs and the environment . It 's projected to grow to be a $3 billion industry , meaning plenty of jobs in handling , transporting and recycling across Australia . "

@@102346794 <h> Green energy : Addressing modern slavery and respecting human rights <p> ESG is transforming society from a global to a local level and every institution needs the right guidance to manage risk , grasp opportunities and navigate change . <p> It 's not just about climate : Renewable energy sources need to respect human rights and build stronger relationships with First Nations communities , as well as helping achieve net zero . <p> As one of the world 's largest corporate law firms , Norton Rose Fulbright has a holistic view of how ESG is changing commercial enterprise across every continent . In this sponsored post , ESG partners , Abigail McGregor and Gavin Scott provide their insight on the below topics in the clean energy sector : <p> Part 1 addressing modern slavery and <p> Part 2 fostering sustainable relationships with First Nations groups <h> The clean energy supply chain <p> With governments and investors around the world scaling up efforts to tackle climate change , the roll-out of renewable energy is on track for a rapid acceleration across the globe . Beyond decarbonisation , this transformation of the energy sector brings significant job creation and investment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ supply chains for renewable energy products to modern slavery . <p> While renewable energy is not unique in this regard -- with long and complex supply chains , the production and distribution of many products in today 's economy are increasingly being linked to modern slavery -- it is an issue that the renewable energy industry must take seriously . <p> The undisputed benefits of clean energy when it comes to climate change do not absolve the industry of its impacts in other areas . <h> Points of exposure <p> The main points of exposure include the manufacture of components and the extraction of raw materials . <p> In some instances , the renewable energy sector is the primary market for products linked to modern slavery ( such as polysilicon , a key component in solar panels ) . In other cases , such as copper , renewable energy is just one of many end uses . <p> For some raw materials , such as cobalt and nickel , renewable energy currently consumes only a small fraction of global output , but the accelerating energy transition will make renewable energy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This creates the potential for the clean energy sector to wield greater influence over the integrity of these supply chains . <h> Geopolitical constraints <p> Challenges of geopolitics and lack of transparency add to the complexity of finding effective strategies for reducing ( and ideally eliminating ) modern slavery from Australia 's clean energy supply chains . This can pose practical limitations for conducting supplier due diligence , where independent auditing of facilities in certain locations is not possible . <p> Carefully considered strategies aimed at reducing or eliminating modern slavery should be developed through collaboration between government agencies , industry and civil society stakeholders . These strategies should also aim to minimise any disruption to the transition to clean energy . <h> Response by business and industry <p> Business entities operating in the renewable energy sector can play an important role in mitigating the risks of modern slavery in their supply chains . It is the responsibility of business enterprises to respect internationally recognised human rights , which would require a business to take adequate measures to prevent , mitigate and account for its impacts . <p> The actions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ depend on a business 's size and leverage . Strategies include the implementation of polices , supplier due diligence measures , remediation processes and stakeholder collaboration . <h> Importance of strong and sustainable relationships with First Nations <p> Gavin Scott , Partner in charge of Native Title and Aboriginal Cultural Heritage for Norton Rose Fulbright Australia recently presented at a global conference in relation to trends in engagement with First Nations groups when developing major projects . <h> When things go wrong ... <p> It is apparent that poor relationships and poor engagement strategies can lead to negative outcomes in the development and operations of projects . These can include protest and media campaigns , actions taken by First Nations groups , mitigation and regulatory enquiries and the associated delays because of those matters . <p> In addition financers , investors and shareholders get extremely concerned about loan documentation conditions in relation to non-compliance with environmental and social impact conditions , and investigations and information requests from institutional investors can ensue . <p> We have also seen a direct rise in shareholder activism at annual general meetings , including removal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ First Nations groups have been tarnished . <p> Regulators are keen to investigate and prosecute for any unlawful impact on First Nations ' rights and interests including where there has been damage to cultural heritage and this can lead to threats of cancellation of approvals , licences and tenures . <p> Panorama of Outback Track in Western Australia . <h> When things go right ... <p> Conversely , when a project actually develops a global best practice standard for engaging with First Nations groups , projects have the space to develop innovative commercial arrangements that can provide ongoing support for the project . <p> Further , these arrangements can helpthe First Nations ' community deliver energy justice to the community and can provide good reputational standing about the relationship with and impact on First Nations . <p> Financiers , investors and shareholders can be satisfied through review of annual compliance reports in relation to social and environmental impacts and regulators will be less likely to have any cause for concern about the project . <h> Moving to better standards of engagement <p> Most successes that we are seeing in this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to go to the highest standard of engagement with First Nations groups . <p> This can be through adherence to numerous international standards such as the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights or the International Finance Corporation Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability . <p> As an example , Performance Standard 7 released by the International Finance Corporation provides for a ready reckoner , step by step process to ensure that engagement with First Nations groups achieves best practice and moves away from base level compliance . The process suggested under that regime includes : <p> adequate resourcing and time to understand the baseline information of the First Nations group that is being impacted ; <p> a very thorough and detailed assessment of the environmental and social impacts that the project may cause ; <p> substantial time to progress through a pre-development consultation process including a plan for future engagement ; <p> developing a strategy to avoid and mitigate negative impacts and more importantly to enhance positive impacts . <p> We are also seeing a lot of time spent on co-existence possibilities so that there is not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the other but indigenous values and corporate values working collaboratively and in partnership . This can include ensuring there are culturally appropriate measures to enhance opportunities and culturally appropriate grievance mechanisms that work when things do not go right . <p> It is important , regardless of the types of guidance used to achieve these best practice standards , that adequate resourcing and time are invested and that there is good evaluation and reporting . <h> Trends in best practice First Nations engagement <p> Some of the key trends that we are seeing in all levels of major projects in relation to First Nations are as follows : <p> More innovative projects are seeing First Nations groups as partners and not just stakeholders . First Nations groups do bring significant value to a project which may include statutory rights , such as the right to give consent to a project , land access rights , knowledge including being able to speak to cultural heritage artefacts and landscapes that might be impacted as well as knowledge of the potential social impact of a project on the wider First Nations groups . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ project can be a very good relationship building step as well as delivering good commercial outcomes for the First Nations group that is impacted by the project . <p> A further innovative step that we have seen in engagements with First Nations groups for early development projects revolves around equity participation . A number of projects in Western Australia have already announced that there is an equity component in relation to agreements with First Nations groups for renewable projects and we see this as something that will continue to grow as more renewable projects engage with First Nations groups as partners . This equity participation can take a number of forms , including as pure compensation under an Indigenous use agreement , or in more sophisticated ways by ensuring that the First Nations group is properly represented in any constitution and shareholders agreement regarding the manner that they hold the equity , the right that that equity brings , dilution rights and impacts on that equity in leading up to and post-Final Investment Decision . <p> For existing projects we are also seeing that there is a drive to review @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ago by the next generation leaders of First Nations ' groups . For projects that are considering agreements , there are opportunities to ensure there are good review processes and to think about intergenerational equity in the development of agreements moving forward . Best practice , by its very nature , continually advances and flexible agreements are now including regular review points to ensure that not only is the agreement working and engagement is still strong and sustainable , but that it also meets current community expectations . <p> Finally . there has been a significant increase in consideration of the manner in which First Nation groups can be engaged in relation to decommissioning , rehabilitation and restoration of landscapes at the conclusion of any renewables project . To date we have not seen a renewable project decommissioned but there is a lot of thought going into how a First Nations group might be engaged in that process , not only for the physical rehabilitation but for issues such as land handbacks and transfers , recycling of used infrastructure and ensuring that the cultural landscape is also " healed " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ request : Listen ! <p> The key aspect for any project that is starting on the journey of engagement with a First Nations group is to really stop and listen to the concerns of the group when developing an engagement plan . This will be for the betterment of the project in the long run . <p> This is a sponsored article written in collaboration with Norton Rose Fulbright Australia and is part of a series on the green energy transition that includes several special podcasts . @@102346795 <p> The rapid growth of wind and solar in Australia and the rest of the world is leading a major structural shift in power sector emissions that point to a significant decline in carbon pollution in come years . <p> A new report by think tank Ember shows the global power sector saw emissions tick up by just 0.2 per cent in the first half of 2023 , compared with the same period last year . <p> Emissions would have fallen significantly , however , were it not for droughts in China that had a major impact on hydro generation . <p> The report suggests 2023 might be the first year of structural power sector emissions reductions , if the growth in renewables continues , as this is the first year when emissions should have fallen , without a " shock " driver like COVID-19 , given the amount of renewables available . <p> Although the world is seeing a rapid adoption of wind and solar , this was countered by falling hydro , as drought in China accounted for 75 per cent of a historic crash in global hydro generation of 177 TWh . <p> Power sector emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hydro generation been at the same level as last year -- but even this picture is complicated . <p> Somewhat offsetting the rise of fossil fuels to fill the hydro deficit was low power demand , as the EU 's energy intensive industries were yet to recover from 2022 's high energy price-induced slump , and other advanced economies also experienced sharp declines in electricity use , according to the International Energy Agency . <p> Yet even as China was supporting its flailing hydro sector with coal and gas , that low demand globally cut into that fossil fuel generation and ameliorated some of the emissions which would otherwise have been created . <p> The Ember report found global electricity demand rose only 0.4 per cent in the first half of 2023 , which is much lower than the 10-year historic average of 2.6 per cent . <h> Fossil fuels still a force to be reckoned with <p> The report did not include specific data for Australia . But the quarterly update of Australia 's National Greenhouse Gas Inventory , released in August , shows emissions trending slightly lower in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sector reductions . <p> Ongoing reductions in emissions from electricity were down 3.9 per cent , or 6.3 Mt CO2-e , for the year to March . <p> In Australia , the numbers were put down to the growth in wind and solar , and particularly rooftop solar . <p> Around the world , the momentum of fossil fuels is still a force to be reckoned with . <p> " It 's still hanging in the balance if 2023 will see a fall in power sector emissions , " said Malgorzata Wiatros-Motyka , the lead author of the report and senior electricity analyst at Ember . <p> " While it is encouraging to see the remarkable growth of wind and solar energy , we ca n't ignore the stark reality of adverse hydro conditions intensified by climate change . <p> " The world is teetering at the peak of power sector emissions , and we now need to unleash the momentum for a rapid decline in fossil fuels by securing a global agreement to triple renewables capacity this decade . " <h> Wind and solar on a tear <p> Ember @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ covers 78 countries . <p> Wind and solar were the only two electricity sources that significantly increased their share of global electricity , together providing 14.3 per cent of global electricity in the first half of 2023 , compared to 12.8 per cent in the same period last year . <p> But the amount of wind and solar added was less than in the same period last year , pointing to a slow down in investment in the sector as the consequences of higher costs and tighter supply chains continue to flow through the sector . <p> Solar in particular is growing quickly , given it can be installed much more quickly and easily than wind turbines , allowing 50 countries to set new monthly records for solar generation in the first half of 2023 . <p> China accounted for 43 per cent of global growth in solar generation , while the EU , US and India accounted for about 12 per cent each . <h> Droughts may be the new normal for hydro <p> The droughts in China meant fossil fuel generators needed to fill the deficit created by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be the new normal for hydro power in southern China . <p> Climate change is causing the tropics to narrow , creating more and more powerful rainfall in that region , while the dry edges expand . <p> What this likely means for Asia is more rain over northern Australia -- and less over southern China , researchers from the Center for Monsoon System Research at the Chinese Academy of Sciences in Beijing suggested in 2017 , particularly during El Nino phenomena .

Another 10 were added by 2004 and four more last year , for a total of 37 . <p> That might not sound a lot but the bar is high when it comes to proving a species has been lost . <p> Actual extinctions could be as many as 222 , given there are another 185 species with no known surviving population . <p> Five Australian species are among the 37 declared gone forever , including Queensland 's northern and southern gastric-brooding frogs - extraordinary creatures that would swallow fertilised eggs to keep them safe before vomiting up live young when the time was right . <p> Australian species last spotted decades ago are also among the 185 whose existence is in serious doubt . <p> Dr Hoskin believes they 're extinct and there 's little that can be done but there are many others that need urgent help , especially critically endangered frogs living on tropical mountain tops . <p> They used to be cool places of refuge but are rapidly warming . Every degree of extra heat forces frogs higher and soon there 'll @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the mountain-top nursery frog epitomises the risk . It lives only on Mount Lewis , west of Port Douglas , in Queensland 's far north . <p> " It 's only found above 1200 metres elevation on a mountain that only goes to about 1350 metres . It 's just pinned right up on top of the highest ridge now . <p> " I would suggest it probably gets the grim title of the species most likely to extinct from climate change in the next years . " <p> With less than 1000 individuals left , no captive breeding program in place and an increasing number of heatwaves affecting the region , his pessimism seems justified . <p> But he also says it 's not too late for that frog and others . <p> " Captive breeding for frogs can take a decade to sort out the methodology . We certainly need to be doing now . In fact we needed it yesterday . " <p> Janice Chanson , who has helped co-ordinate the two amphibian assessments , says there 's still time for the federal government to meet its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ long-lasting drought might be enough to rob some endangered species of the streams they need to breed or bushfires could fill those streams with ash and that could be the nail in the coffin . <p> " Australia is very good at documenting extinctions and not necessarily doing anything to stop them , " she says .

@@102347858 <h> Opinion <h> ' Planetary boundaries ' set the limits of economic freedom <h> Save articles for later <p> Add articles to your saved list and come back to them any time . <p> Advertisement <p> One of the most important developments in economics is something in which economists had no hand : the identification of the environmental limits which humans , busily producing and consuming , cross at their peril . <p> Earth has existed for about 4 billion years and humans have lived on Earth for about 200,000 years . For almost all of that time we were hunters and gatherers , but 10,000 to 12,000 years ago we settled down , to farm and create civilisation . <p> Credit : <p> It 's probably no coincidence that , for about that time , Earth has enjoyed a stable climate , with no more ice ages nor period of great heat , in which palms grew in Antarctica . This is the geological epoch called the Holocene , in which we live -- although it may be ruled that we 've moved to the Anthropocene , a new epoch in which the human species has made major @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , economics can be dated to 1776 , when Adam Smith published The Wealth of Nations . Beliefs about how the economy works were well-defined by the time Alfred Marshall published Principles of Economics in 1890 . <p> The point is that all economic activity -- all the efforts of humans to earn a living -- both depends on the natural environment and adversely affects it . By 1900 , there were only about 1.6 billion humans on the planet , not enough to do much damage . <p> If we wrecked some area , we could just move to somewhere that had n't been wrecked , while the first bit gradually recovered . <p> The main thing that 's changed is our use of advances in technology to hugely increase our standard of living and , in the process , massively increase the damage we 're doing to the environment . <p> So , at the time conventional economics was established , it was perfectly sensible to assume that the environment 's role in economic activity could be taken for granted . It was just there and it always @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a " free good " . <p> When , from the 1700s , we started burning fossil fuel -- coal , oil and gas -- for heat , light and energy , we had no reason to worry that one day it might run out . It certainly never occurred to us that this might end up having an effect on the climate . <p> Advertisement <p> It took many decades before scientists began telling us that all the things we were doing to improve our lives -- cutting down forests , damming rivers , drilling for water , ploughing , fertilising crops , fishing with nets -- were damaging the soil , causing erosion , killing species , lowering the water table , and damaging the environment in other ways . <p> However , in just the past century or so , the world 's population has gone from 1.6 billion to 8 billion . Every extra human does a bit more damage to the environment . But that 's not the main thing . The main thing that 's changed is our use of advances in technology to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ process , massively increase the damage we 're doing to the environment . <p> Loading <p> Which brings us to " planetary boundaries " . In 2009 , the Swedish scientist Johan Rockstrom and a scientist from the Australian National University , the late Will Steffen , with many helpers , established a framework listing the key categories of environmental damage , and estimating the amount of damage that could be done to each before the risk increased that " the Earth system " could no longer recover . <p> A second update of these estimates , led by an American oceanographer based in Copenhagen , Katherine Richardson , was released last month . With the ANU 's Professor Xuemei Bai , Richardson has written an article explaining the planetary boundaries . <p> There are nine boundaries . Three of them cover what we take from the ecological system : loss of biodiversity ( extinction of species ) , loss of fresh water ( pumping too much water from rivers and aquifers ) and land use ( deforestation ) . <p> Something economists did n't know -- or did n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ say we can never truly get rid of anything that exists on Earth . <p> All we -- or the ecosystem -- can do is change the form of the thing . Water can evaporate , but it 's still up in the clouds , for instance . We can cut down a tree , but as it slowly sinks into the dirt , it releases the carbon dioxide it had previously taken up . <p> This means that all our economic activity leaves in its wake a lot of waste . Not just landfill , but in many other forms . <p> So , the remaining six boundaries concern the waste our activity greatly adds to what would have occurred naturally . They are : greenhouse gases which cause climate change ; ocean acidification ( carbon absorbed by the sea ) ; emission of chemicals that deplete the Earth 's ozone layer ; " novel entities " ( synthetic chemicals such as plastics , DDT and concrete ) ; aerosols ; and nutrient overload ( nitrogen and phosphorus from fertilisers that wash into rivers and the sea , causing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Crossing any of these boundaries does n't trigger immediate disaster . But it does mean we 've moved from the safe zone into dangerous territory . And the nine boundaries are interrelated and interacting , in ways we do n't yet fully understand . <p> In 2009 , the scientists found we 'd already crossed three boundaries : biodiversity , climate change and nutrient overload . By the 2015 update , a fourth boundary had been crossed : land use . <p> And by this year 's update , only three boundaries had n't been crossed : ocean acidification ( but only just ) , aerosol pollution , and stratospheric ozone depletion -- where an international agreement banning CFCs is slowly reducing the ozone hole we created . <p> Richardson and Bai say we 're now well into the danger zone , " where we -- as well as every other species -- are now at risk " . " We are eating away at our own life support systems , " they say . <p> One thing to be said for economists is that , unlike some , they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ job . Very few economists dispute the scientists ' evidence that climate change has been caused by human activities . <p> It was economists who developed the best means to reduce carbon emissions -- emission trading schemes -- which other countries have adopted , but Australia rejected . <p> When our governments decide to act on the other planetary boundaries , it will be economists who work out the best way to do it .

This meeting will provide a platform for the region 's energy ministers to plan a regional agenda for a sustainable energy transition . <p> Looming large among these issues is the lack of access to electricity and clean cooking fuels for hundreds of millions of people . This deprivation has far-reaching consequences and is a harsh reminder @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economic development , not everyone has enjoyed the fruits of progress . <p> Lack of access to electricity hinders healthcare , education and economic opportunities . Moreover , the reliance on traditional cooking fuels such as fuelwood contributes to respiratory diseases that disproportionately affect women and children . Energy poverty exacerbates existing inequalities , trapping communities in a cycle of deprivation . <p> Realising the SDG 7 targets requires increased financial flows . According to the Secretary-General 's Global Roadmap for Accelerated SDG Action , annual investments in access to electricity must increase by $35 billion and by $25 billion for clean cooking by 2025 . A tripling of renewable energy and energy efficiency investment is needed by 2030 . <p> Scaling up finance at this rate requires a large infusion of private finance to bolster insufficient public sources , alongside a shifting of national budgets away from fossil fuels . Carbon pricing mechanisms can incentivise businesses to transition towards cleaner energy solutions . Innovative business models and financial instruments can attract international finance . But for these to be successful , governments must provide predictable and enabling policy environments @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over the long term , governments must keep an eye on over-the-horizon risks . Key among these is ensuring adequate , stable and predictable supplies of critical raw materials needed to construct the millions of solar panels , wind turbines and batteries of the future . <p> Our region holds immense potential for critical raw materials production , making it a key player in the global energy transition . However , regional collaboration is needed alongside responsible mining and extraction practices that minimise environmental damage and social disruptions . Moreover , investing in the recycling of critical raw materials can reduce our consumption of finite resources . <p> While transitioning towards clean energy is a moral and environmental imperative , a just transition ensures that no one is left behind as countries move away from fossil fuels and towards sustainable resources and technologies . This includes reskilling and reemployment opportunities for workers in declining industries , as well as community support to mitigate the socio-economic impacts of the energy transition . <p> Achieving SDG 7 requires a multifaceted approach . This is not a challenge that any one country or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ innovation and shared responsibility . As we reflect on our progress at this halfway point , it is timely for countries across Asia and the Pacific to recommit to a regional vision where all citizens have access to clean and modern energy and the full potential of renewables and energy efficiency are realised . <p> The momentum behind these changes is growing and the opportunity to close these gaps must be seized .
